Well, I suppose the main thing is I’ve noticed I can’t seem to stay seated when I’m meant to. It feels like I need to do something, even though when I think about it, it’s not really urgent.\n\nI’m a teacher, so meetings are part of life, unfortunately—sorry, that’s not really the point—but it’s been happening enough that I’ve started wondering why I keep doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring thoughts that you can't seem to get out of your head?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you mean, anyway. I mean, I’ll have a lot of things bouncing around in my head, especially school things—marking, emails, who needs what form signed—but I wouldn’t say it’s one thought that gets stuck and won’t leave.\n\nThough now I’m wondering if you mean worries? Because I can get preoccupied with little practical things, like whether I remembered to lock the classroom cupboard, but then I’ll usually move on to something else. Sorry, I’m not sure if I’m answering that properly.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or on edge?\n\nPatient: Anxious… I don’t know, not especially? I mean, everyone at school is a bit on edge at certain times—exam season, inspections, when the photocopier jams five minutes before a lesson, which is practically a weekly event—but I wouldn’t say I walk around feeling anxious all the time.\n\nI can get a bit keyed up, I suppose, but I’m not sure if that’s anxiety or just being busy. What do you mean by “on edge,” exactly?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do things in a certain way or repeat certain actions?\n\nPatient: Not really, I don’t think so. Yes, I suppose I do—though not in a dramatic way. It’s more in conversations. Like at book club, or curriculum meetings at school, I’ll suddenly jump in before someone’s actually finished what they’re saying because the answer is already there in my head and out it comes.

interrupts or intrudes on others (e.g., butts into conversations, games, or activities).\n  manifestation: In group discussions I often jump in before someone has finished their thought, especially at know, not especially? I mean, everyone at school is a bit on edge at certain times—exam season, inspections, when the photocopier jams five minutes before a lesson, which is practically a weekly event—but I wouldn’t say I walk around feeling anxious all the time.\n\nI can get a bit keyed up, I suppose, but I’m not sure if that’s anxiety or just being busy. What do you mean by “on edge,” exactly?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do things in a certain way or repeat certain actions?\n\nPatient: Not really, I don’t think so. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, I do, actually. Not constantly, but enough that I notice it. If I’m sitting at my desk marking essays, I’ll realise my foot’s been tapping away for ages, or I’m clicking my pen without thinking—which is dreadful if someone else is in the room, because it’s the sort of thing that would drive me mad if a pupil were doing it.

And in church, when I’m meant to be sitting still and listening properly, I’ll find I’m shifting about in the pew or moving my feet, and then I sort of tell myself, “Right, settle down.” It’s not deliberate, it just seems to happen when I’m sitting still for too long.

If I’m sitting at my desk marking essays, I’ll realise my foot’s been tapping away for ages, or I’m clicking my pen without thinking—which is dreadful if someone else is in the room, because it’s the sort of thing that would drive me mad if a pupil were doing it.\n\nAnd in church, when I’m meant to be sitting still and listening properly, I’ll find I’m shifting about in the pew or moving my feet, and then I sort of tell myself, “Right, settle down.” It’s not deliberate, it just seems to happen when I’m sitting still for too long.\n\n[Last Question]\nAre these behaviors, like getting up during meetings or fidgeting, causing problems for you at work or in your relationships with others?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 60 / secondary_school_teacher / graduate\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Functional Impairment\nmanifestation: \"It has started to affect my relationships at work and in my community because people lose patience with me in meetings and book club, and I leave feeling embarrassed instead of connected.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I think it has started to, a bit. Not in a catastrophic way, but people do lose patience with me sometimes, especially in meetings at work. I can see it on their faces when I’ve jumped in again or when I’m shifting about instead of just sitting quietly.

And at book club too, which sounds silly, because that’s meant to be enjoyable—we were discussing a novel last month and I kept coming in too quickly, and afterwards I went home feeling embarrassed rather than, you know, part of things. Connected, I suppose. Same with people in the community sometimes. I don’t mean any harm, but I can leave feeling I’ve irritated everyone.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, I think it does, though I almost feel silly saying that because it’s not as if anything dreadful has happened. But it does wear on me.

It’s more the after-effect, really. I’ll come away from a staff meeting or book club thinking, “Why did I say that like that?” or “Why couldn’t I just wait?” And then I feel embarrassed, and a bit separate from people, instead of feeling I’ve joined in properly. People do lose patience with me sometimes — not always openly, but you can tell, can’t you?

It’s affected things at work and in the community a bit, I’d say. I used to really enjoy those sorts of discussions, and I still do, but now I sometimes dread that I’ll irritate everyone again.

I mean, I’ll have a lot of things bouncing around in my head, especially school things—marking, emails, who needs what form signed—but I wouldn’t say it’s one thought that gets stuck and won’t leave.\n\nThough now I’m wondering if you mean worries? Because I can get preoccupied with little practical things, like whether I remembered to lock the classroom cupboard, but then I’ll usually move on to something else. Sorry, I’m not sure if I’m answering that properly.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or on edge?\n\nPatient: Anxious… I don’t know, not especially? I mean, everyone at school is a bit on edge at certain times—exam season, inspections, when the photocopier jams five minutes before a lesson, which is practically a weekly event—but I wouldn’t say I walk around feeling anxious all the time.\n\nI can get a bit keyed up, I suppose, but I’m not sure if that’s anxiety or just being busy. What do you mean by “on edge,” exactly?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do things in a certain way or repeat certain actions?\n\nPatient: Not really, I don’t think so. I mean, I have routines, like most teachers do—if you don’t have some sort of system, the whole day falls apart, especially with thirty pupils all needing something at once—but I wouldn’t say I have to do things a certain way or repeat things.\n\nI might double-check I’ve got my keys or my register, but that feels more like being practical than anything. Sorry, I’ve gone off a bit. No, I haven’t noticed any repeating actions like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose I do—though not in a dramatic way. It’s more in conversations. Like at book club, or curriculum meetings at school, I’ll suddenly jump in before someone’s actually finished what they’re saying because the answer is already there in my head and out it comes.\n\nIt’s embarrassing sometimes, because I don’t mean to be rude. I’ll think, “Oh, for goodness’ sake, let them finish,” but by then I’ve already said it. It happens more when I’m interested in the topic, actually—if we’re discussing a novel or planning a scheme of work, I get carried along with it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting with your hands or feet when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do, actually. Not constantly, but enough that I notice it. If I’m sitting at my desk marking essays, I’ll realise my foot’s been tapping away for ages, or I’m clicking my pen without thinking—which is dreadful if someone else is in the room, because it’s the sort of thing that would drive me mad if a pupil were doing it.\n\nAnd in church, when I’m meant to be sitting still and listening properly, I’ll find I’m shifting about in the pew or moving my feet, and then I sort of tell myself, “Right, settle down.” It’s not deliberate, it just seems to happen when I’m sitting still for too long.\n\nDoctor: Are these behaviors, like getting up during meetings or fidgeting, causing problems for you at work or in your relationships with others?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it has started to, a bit. Not in a catastrophic way, but people do lose patience with me sometimes, especially in meetings at work. I can see it on their faces when I’ve jumped in again or when I’m shifting about instead of just sitting quietly.\n\nAnd at book club too, which sounds silly, because that’s meant to be enjoyable—we were discussing a novel last month and I kept coming in too quickly, and afterwards I went home feeling embarrassed rather than, you know, part of things. Connected, I suppose. Same with people in the community sometimes. I don’t mean any harm, but I can leave feeling I’ve irritated everyone.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these behaviors are affecting your overall well-being or causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it does, though I almost feel silly saying that because it’s not as if anything dreadful has happened. But it does wear on me.\n\nIt’s more the after-effect, really. I’ll come away from a staff meeting or book club thinking, “Why did I say that like that?” or “Why couldn’t I just wait?” And then I feel embarrassed, and a bit separate from people, instead of feeling I’ve joined in properly. People do lose patience with me sometimes — not always openly, but you can tell, can’t you?\n\nIt’s affected things at work and in the community a bit, I’d say. I used to really enjoy those sorts of discussions, and I still do, but now I sometimes dread that I’ll irritate everyone again.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

I’m not sure, actually. I mean, with lesson plans and marking, I do finish them because one has to, don’t you? The pupils are coming in whether I’m ready or not, and the essays can’t mark themselves—more’s the pity.

I suppose I can get a bit waylaid, but I don’t know if it’s more than anyone else. Sometimes I’ll sit down to do one thing and end up sorting a pile of papers or remembering I need to email someone about a school trip—well, not even a trip, more like one of those assemblies we all get roped into—and then I think, “What was I meant to be doing?”

But I wouldn’t want to say I often don’t follow through on instructions. I’m not sure that’s the main thing, really.

I can see it on their faces when I’ve jumped in again or when I’m shifting about instead of just sitting quietly.\n\nAnd at book club too, which sounds silly, because that’s meant to be enjoyable—we were discussing a novel last month and I kept coming in too quickly, and afterwards I went home feeling embarrassed rather than, you know, part of things. Connected, I suppose. Same with people in the community sometimes. I don’t mean any harm, but I can leave feeling I’ve irritated everyone.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these behaviors are affecting your overall well-being or causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it does, though I almost feel silly saying that because it’s not as if anything dreadful has happened. But it does wear on me.\n\nIt’s more the after-effect, really. I’ll come away from a staff meeting or book club thinking, “Why did I say that like that?” or “Why couldn’t I just wait?” And then I feel embarrassed, and a bit separate from people, instead of feeling I’ve joined in properly. People do lose patience with me sometimes — not always openly, but you can tell, can’t you?\n\nIt’s affected things at work and in the community a bit, I’d say. I used to really enjoy those sorts of discussions, and I still do, but now I sometimes dread that I’ll irritate everyone again.\n\nDoctor: Do you often struggle to follow through on instructions or finish tasks, such as lesson plans or grading assignments?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, actually. I mean, with lesson plans and marking, I do finish them because one has to, don’t you? The pupils are coming in whether I’m ready or not, and the essays can’t mark themselves—more’s the pity.\n\nI suppose I can get a bit waylaid, but I don’t know if it’s more than anyone else. Sometimes I’ll sit down to do one thing and end up sorting a pile of papers or remembering I need to email someone about a school trip—well, not even a trip, more like one of those assemblies we all get roped into—and then I think, “What was I meant to be doing?”\n\nBut I wouldn’t want to say I often don’t follow through on instructions. I’m not sure that’s the main thing, really.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.   },

I’m not sure, really. I mean, I notice things going on around me — in a classroom you rather have to, don’t you? A child rummaging in a bag, someone at the window, the radiator making that dreadful clanking noise we still haven’t had fixed.

But whether I’m more distracted than other people… I don’t know. I haven’t particularly thought of it that way. Sometimes I’ll lose my thread if there’s a lot happening, but then in school there always is a lot happening. Even the corridors have their own weather, almost — noise, coats everywhere, someone needing a form signed.

So… I’m not sure I’d say it’s a big thing I’ve noticed, no.

I used to really enjoy those sorts of discussions, and I still do, but now I sometimes dread that I’ll irritate everyone again.\n\nDoctor: Do you often struggle to follow through on instructions or finish tasks, such as lesson plans or grading assignments?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, actually. I mean, with lesson plans and marking, I do finish them because one has to, don’t you? Sometimes I’ll sit down to do one thing and end up sorting a pile of papers or remembering I need to email someone about a school trip—well, not even a trip, more like one of those assemblies we all get roped into—and then I think, “What was I meant to be doing?”\n\nBut I wouldn’t want to say I often don’t follow through on instructions. I’m not sure that’s the main thing, really.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted by things around you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, really. I mean, I notice things going on around me — in a classroom you rather have to, don’t you? I haven’t particularly thought of it that way. Sometimes I’ll lose my thread if there’s a lot happening, but then in school there always is a lot happening. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I think I do, actually. Not all the time, but I’ve noticed it particularly in the Spanish department, or at the cultural centre, where we’ll be discussing something quite ordinary and I somehow end up giving three examples when one would have done.

It’s funny, because I don’t set out to dominate anything. I just get caught up — like if we’re talking about a film screening, I’ll start mentioning the director, and then something about an article I read, and then a student who once said something relevant, and by then I can see someone glancing at their watch or doing that polite little “right, anyway” face.

And then afterwards I feel embarrassed. I think, “Why didn’t I just stop sooner?” So yes, I suppose I can take over a conversation more than I mean to.

People do lose patience with me sometimes — not always openly, but you can tell, can’t you?\n\nIt’s affected things at work and in the community a bit, I’d say. I used to really enjoy those sorts of discussions, and I still do, but now I sometimes dread that I’ll irritate everyone again.\n\nDoctor: Do you often struggle to follow through on instructions or finish tasks, such as lesson plans or grading assignments?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, actually. I mean, with lesson plans and marking, I do finish them because one has to, don’t you? The pupils are coming in whether I’m ready or not, and the essays can’t mark themselves—more’s the pity.\n\nI suppose I can get a bit waylaid, but I don’t know if it’s more than anyone else. Sometimes I’ll sit down to do one thing and end up sorting a pile of papers or remembering I need to email someone about a school trip—well, not even a trip, more like one of those assemblies we all get roped into—and then I think, “What was I meant to be doing?”\n\nBut I wouldn’t want to say I often don’t follow through on instructions. I’m not sure that’s the main thing, really.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted by things around you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, really. I mean, I notice things going on around me — in a classroom you rather have to, don’t you? A child rummaging in a bag, someone at the window, the radiator making that dreadful clanking noise we still haven’t had fixed.\n\nBut whether I’m more distracted than other people… I don’t know. I haven’t particularly thought of it that way. Sometimes I’ll lose my thread if there’s a lot happening, but then in school there always is a lot happening. Even the corridors have their own weather, almost — noise, coats everywhere, someone needing a form signed.\n\nSo… I’m not sure I’d say it’s a big thing I’ve noticed, no.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself talking more than others in social situations?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I do, actually. Not all the time, but I’ve noticed it particularly in the Spanish department, or at the cultural centre, where we’ll be discussing something quite ordinary and I somehow end up giving three examples when one would have done.\n\nIt’s funny, because I don’t set out to dominate anything. I just get caught up — like if we’re talking about a film screening, I’ll start mentioning the director, and then something about an article I read, and then a student who once said something relevant, and by then I can see someone glancing at their watch or doing that polite little “right, anyway” face.\n\nAnd then afterwards I feel embarrassed. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think it’s more a restlessness thing, probably. It’s not always that I’ve stopped listening exactly — though I suppose if I’m walking to the back of the room, I’m not fully sitting with it, am I?

It’s more that in meetings, or even at dinner when everyone else is perfectly settled, I suddenly feel I ought to get up and do something. Refill the coffee, check whether there are enough handouts, move something at the side of the room… and then afterwards I think, “Was that actually necessary?” Usually it wasn’t urgent at all.

It’s a bit embarrassing, really, because other people can just stay put, and I’m up and down like I’m hosting a village fête.

If I’m sitting at my desk marking essays, I’ll realise my foot’s been tapping away for ages, or I’m clicking my pen without thinking—which is dreadful if someone else is in the room, because it’s the sort of thing that would drive me mad if a pupil were doing it.\n\nAnd in church, when I’m meant to be sitting still and listening properly, I’ll find I’m shifting about in the pew or moving my feet, and then I sort of tell myself, “Right, settle down.” It’s not deliberate, it just seems to happen when I’m sitting still for too long.\n\nDoctor: Are these behaviors, like getting up during meetings or fidgeting, causing problems for you at work or in your relationships with others?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it has started to, a bit. Not in a catastrophic way, but people do lose patience with me sometimes, especially in meetings at work. I can see it on their faces when I’ve jumped in again or when I’m shifting about instead of just sitting quietly.\n\nAnd at book club too, which sounds silly, because that’s meant to be enjoyable—we were discussing a novel last month and I kept coming in too quickly, and afterwards I went home feeling embarrassed rather than, you know, part of things. Connected, I suppose. Same with people in the community sometimes. I don’t mean any harm, but I can leave feeling I’ve irritated everyone.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these behaviors are affecting your overall well-being or causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it does, though I almost feel silly saying that because it’s not as if anything dreadful has happened. But it does wear on me.\n\nIt’s more the after-effect, really. I’ll come away from a staff meeting or book club thinking, “Why did I say that like that?” or “Why couldn’t I just wait?” And then I feel embarrassed, and a bit separate from people, instead of feeling I’ve joined in properly. People do lose patience with me sometimes — not always openly, but you can tell, can’t you?\n\nIt’s affected things at work and in the community a bit, I’d say. I used to really enjoy those sorts of discussions, and I still do, but now I sometimes dread that I’ll irritate everyone again.\n\nDoctor: Do you often struggle to follow through on instructions or finish tasks, such as lesson plans or grading assignments?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, actually. I mean, with lesson plans and marking, I do finish them because one has to, don’t you? The pupils are coming in whether I’m ready or not, and the essays can’t mark themselves—more’s the pity.\n\nI suppose I can get a bit waylaid, but I don’t know if it’s more than anyone else. Sometimes I’ll sit down to do one thing and end up sorting a pile of papers or remembering I need to email someone about a school trip—well, not even a trip, more like one of those assemblies we all get roped into—and then I think, “What was I meant to be doing?”\n\nBut I wouldn’t want to say I often don’t follow through on instructions. I’m not sure that’s the main thing, really.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted by things around you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, really. I mean, I notice things going on around me — in a classroom you rather have to, don’t you? A child rummaging in a bag, someone at the window, the radiator making that dreadful clanking noise we still haven’t had fixed.\n\nBut whether I’m more distracted than other people… I don’t know. I haven’t particularly thought of it that way. Sometimes I’ll lose my thread if there’s a lot happening, but then in school there always is a lot happening. Even the corridors have their own weather, almost — noise, coats everywhere, someone needing a form signed.\n\nSo… I’m not sure I’d say it’s a big thing I’ve noticed, no.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself talking more than others in social situations?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I do, actually. Not all the time, but I’ve noticed it particularly in the Spanish department, or at the cultural centre, where we’ll be discussing something quite ordinary and I somehow end up giving three examples when one would have done.\n\nIt’s funny, because I don’t set out to dominate anything. I just get caught up — like if we’re talking about a film screening, I’ll start mentioning the director, and then something about an article I read, and then a student who once said something relevant, and by then I can see someone glancing at their watch or doing that polite little “right, anyway” face.\n\nAnd then afterwards I feel embarrassed. I think, “Why didn’t I just stop sooner?” So yes, I suppose I can take over a conversation more than I mean to.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty staying seated and fidgeting is more related to feeling restless or having trouble paying attention?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more a restlessness thing, probably. It’s not always that I’ve stopped listening exactly — though I suppose if I’m walking to the back of the room, I’m not fully sitting with it, am I?\n\nIt’s more that in meetings, or even at dinner when everyone else is perfectly settled, I suddenly feel I ought to get up and do something. Refill the coffee, check whether there are enough handouts, move something at the side of the room… and then afterwards I think, “Was that actually necessary?” Usually it wasn’t urgent at all.\n\nIt’s a bit embarrassing, really, because other people can just stay put, and I’m up and down like I’m hosting a village fête.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I’m not sure I’d call them impulsive decisions, exactly. I mean, I can decide things rather quickly sometimes — in a staffroom sort of way, you know, “Yes, let’s move that lesson to Thursday,” or “Oh, I’ll volunteer for that,” and then later I remember my diary is already full of tiny little traps.

But serious decisions… I don’t think I’m reckless. I’m not off buying cars or booking flights to Peru on a whim — though Peru would be wonderful, wouldn’t it, all those textiles and the mountains. We did a unit once where a student brought in something her grandmother had woven, and I remember thinking—

Sorry, I’m wandering. I suppose I haven’t really noticed myself making big decisions without thinking through the consequences. Smaller things, perhaps, but I don’t know that it’s a major problem.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.8, F90.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose the main thing is I’ve noticed I can’t seem to stay seated when I’m meant to. Like in staff meetings, or even at dinner, everyone else is sitting there and I’ll suddenly get up to refill coffee, or check a handout, or wander to the back of the room. It feels like I need to do something, even though when I think about it, it’s not really urgent.\n\nI’m a teacher, so meetings are part of life, unfortunately—sorry, that’s not really the point—but it’s been happening enough that I’ve started wondering why I keep doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring thoughts that you can't seem to get out of your head?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you mean, anyway. I mean, I’ll have a lot of things bouncing around in my head, especially school things—marking, emails, who needs what form signed—but I wouldn’t say it’s one thought that gets stuck and won’t leave.\n\nThough now I’m wondering if you mean worries? Because I can get preoccupied with little practical things, like whether I remembered to lock the classroom cupboard, but then I’ll usually move on to something else. Sorry, I’m not sure if I’m answering that properly.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or on edge?\n\nPatient: Anxious… I don’t know, not especially? I mean, everyone at school is a bit on edge at certain times—exam season, inspections, when the photocopier jams five minutes before a lesson, which is practically a weekly event—but I wouldn’t say I walk around feeling anxious all the time.\n\nI can get a bit keyed up, I suppose, but I’m not sure if that’s anxiety or just being busy. What do you mean by “on edge,” exactly?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do things in a certain way or repeat certain actions?\n\nPatient: Not really, I don’t think so. I mean, I have routines, like most teachers do—if you don’t have some sort of system, the whole day falls apart, especially with thirty pupils all needing something at once—but I wouldn’t say I have to do things a certain way or repeat things.\n\nI might double-check I’ve got my keys or my register, but that feels more like being practical than anything. Sorry, I’ve gone off a bit. No, I haven’t noticed any repeating actions like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose I do—though not in a dramatic way. It’s more in conversations. Like at book club, or curriculum meetings at school, I’ll suddenly jump in before someone’s actually finished what they’re saying because the answer is already there in my head and out it comes.\n\nIt’s embarrassing sometimes, because I don’t mean to be rude. I’ll think, “Oh, for goodness’ sake, let them finish,” but by then I’ve already said it. It happens more when I’m interested in the topic, actually—if we’re discussing a novel or planning a scheme of work, I get carried along with it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting with your hands or feet when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do, actually. Not constantly, but enough that I notice it. If I’m sitting at my desk marking essays, I’ll realise my foot’s been tapping away for ages, or I’m clicking my pen without thinking—which is dreadful if someone else is in the room, because it’s the sort of thing that would drive me mad if a pupil were doing it.\n\nAnd in church, when I’m meant to be sitting still and listening properly, I’ll find I’m shifting about in the pew or moving my feet, and then I sort of tell myself, “Right, settle down.” It’s not deliberate, it just seems to happen when I’m sitting still for too long.\n\nDoctor: Are these behaviors, like getting up during meetings or fidgeting, causing problems for you at work or in your relationships with others?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it has started to, a bit. Not in a catastrophic way, but people do lose patience with me sometimes, especially in meetings at work. I can see it on their faces when I’ve jumped in again or when I’m shifting about instead of just sitting quietly.\n\nAnd at book club too, which sounds silly, because that’s meant to be enjoyable—we were discussing a novel last month and I kept coming in too quickly, and afterwards I went home feeling embarrassed rather than, you know, part of things. Connected, I suppose. Same with people in the community sometimes. I don’t mean any harm, but I can leave feeling I’ve irritated everyone.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these behaviors are affecting your overall well-being or causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it does, though I almost feel silly saying that because it’s not as if anything dreadful has happened. But it does wear on me.\n\nIt’s more the after-effect, really. I’ll come away from a staff meeting or book club thinking, “Why did I say that like that?” or “Why couldn’t I just wait?” And then I feel embarrassed, and a bit separate from people, instead of feeling I’ve joined in properly. People do lose patience with me sometimes — not always openly, but you can tell, can’t you?\n\nIt’s affected things at work and in the community a bit, I’d say. I used to really enjoy those sorts of discussions, and I still do, but now I sometimes dread that I’ll irritate everyone again.\n\nDoctor: Do you often struggle to follow through on instructions or finish tasks, such as lesson plans or grading assignments?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, actually. I mean, with lesson plans and marking, I do finish them because one has to, don’t you? The pupils are coming in whether I’m ready or not, and the essays can’t mark themselves—more’s the pity.\n\nI suppose I can get a bit waylaid, but I don’t know if it’s more than anyone else. Sometimes I’ll sit down to do one thing and end up sorting a pile of papers or remembering I need to email someone about a school trip—well, not even a trip, more like one of those assemblies we all get roped into—and then I think, “What was I meant to be doing?”\n\nBut I wouldn’t want to say I often don’t follow through on instructions. I’m not sure that’s the main thing, really.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted by things around you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, really. I mean, I notice things going on around me — in a classroom you rather have to, don’t you? A child rummaging in a bag, someone at the window, the radiator making that dreadful clanking noise we still haven’t had fixed.\n\nBut whether I’m more distracted than other people… I don’t know. I haven’t particularly thought of it that way. Sometimes I’ll lose my thread if there’s a lot happening, but then in school there always is a lot happening. Even the corridors have their own weather, almost — noise, coats everywhere, someone needing a form signed.\n\nSo… I’m not sure I’d say it’s a big thing I’ve noticed, no.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself talking more than others in social situations?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I do, actually. Not all the time, but I’ve noticed it particularly in the Spanish department, or at the cultural centre, where we’ll be discussing something quite ordinary and I somehow end up giving three examples when one would have done.\n\nIt’s funny, because I don’t set out to dominate anything. I just get caught up — like if we’re talking about a film screening, I’ll start mentioning the director, and then something about an article I read, and then a student who once said something relevant, and by then I can see someone glancing at their watch or doing that polite little “right, anyway” face.\n\nAnd then afterwards I feel embarrassed. I think, “Why didn’t I just stop sooner?” So yes, I suppose I can take over a conversation more than I mean to.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty staying seated and fidgeting is more related to feeling restless or having trouble paying attention?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more a restlessness thing, probably. It’s not always that I’ve stopped listening exactly — though I suppose if I’m walking to the back of the room, I’m not fully sitting with it, am I?\n\nIt’s more that in meetings, or even at dinner when everyone else is perfectly settled, I suddenly feel I ought to get up and do something. Refill the coffee, check whether there are enough handouts, move something at the side of the room… and then afterwards I think, “Was that actually necessary?” Usually it wasn’t urgent at all.\n\nIt’s a bit embarrassing, really, because other people can just stay put, and I’m up and down like I’m hosting a village fête.\n\nDoctor: Do you often make impulsive decisions without fully considering the consequences?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call them impulsive decisions, exactly. I mean, I can decide things rather quickly sometimes — in a staffroom sort of way, you know, “Yes, let’s move that lesson to Thursday,” or “Oh, I’ll volunteer for that,” and then later I remember my diary is already full of tiny little traps.\n\nBut serious decisions… I don’t think I’m reckless. I’m not off buying cars or booking flights to Peru on a whim — though Peru would be wonderful, wouldn’t it, all those textiles and the mountains. We did a unit once where a student brought in something her grandmother had woven, and I remember thinking—\n\nSorry, I’m wandering. I suppose I haven’t really noticed myself making big decisions without thinking through the consequences. Smaller things, perhaps, but I don’t know that it’s a major problem.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

I think, “Why didn’t I just stop sooner?” So yes, I suppose I can take over a conversation more than I mean to.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty staying seated and fidgeting is more related to feeling restless or having trouble paying attention?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more a restlessness thing, probably. Refill the coffee, check whether there are enough handouts, move something at the side of the room… and then afterwards I think, “Was that actually necessary?” Usually it wasn’t urgent at all.\n\nIt’s a bit embarrassing, really, because other people can just stay put, and I’m up and down like I’m hosting a village fête.\n\nDoctor: Do you often make impulsive decisions without fully considering the consequences?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call them impulsive decisions, exactly. I mean, I can decide things rather quickly sometimes — in a staffroom sort of way, you know, “Yes, let’s move that lesson to Thursday,” or “Oh, I’ll volunteer for that,” and then later I remember my diary is already full of tiny little traps.\n\nBut serious decisions… I don’t think I’m reckless. I’m not off buying cars or booking flights to Peru on a whim — though Peru would be wonderful, wouldn’t it, all those textiles and the mountains. We did a unit once where a student brought in something her grandmother had woven, and I remember thinking—\n\nSorry, I’m wandering. I suppose I haven’t really noticed myself making big decisions without thinking through the consequences. Smaller things, perhaps, but I don’t know that it’s a major problem.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think you have more trouble with staying focused on tasks or with sitting still and being patient?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 60 / secondary_school_teacher / graduate\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Leaving_Seat\nmanifestation: \"What brought me in is that I keep getting up in meetings or at dinner when everyone else is seated, like I'll suddenly need to refill coffee, check a handout, or walk to the back of the room even though nothing is really urgent.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think it’s more the sitting still and being patient side of it. I can focus, at least sometimes, if I’m properly engaged, but what brought me in really was this business of getting up when everyone else is seated.

In meetings I’ll suddenly think, “Oh, I should refill the coffee,” or “I’d better check those handouts,” and I’m halfway out of my chair before I’ve really considered whether it needs doing. Or at dinner, I’ll pop up to move something or fetch something, and everyone else is just… calmly sitting there like normal adults.

Sometimes I’ll walk to the back of the room as if there’s a purpose, but there often isn’t anything urgent. It’s more like I can’t quite remain planted. Then afterwards I feel a bit silly, because it probably could have waited.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose the main thing is I’ve noticed I can’t seem to stay seated when I’m meant to. Like in staff meetings, or even at dinner, everyone else is sitting there and I’ll suddenly get up to refill coffee, or check a handout, or wander to the back of the room. It feels like I need to do something, even though when I think about it, it’s not really urgent.\n\nI’m a teacher, so meetings are part of life, unfortunately—sorry, that’s not really the point—but it’s been happening enough that I’ve started wondering why I keep doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring thoughts that you can't seem to get out of your head?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you mean, anyway. I mean, I’ll have a lot of things bouncing around in my head, especially school things—marking, emails, who needs what form signed—but I wouldn’t say it’s one thought that gets stuck and won’t leave.\n\nThough now I’m wondering if you mean worries? Because I can get preoccupied with little practical things, like whether I remembered to lock the classroom cupboard, but then I’ll usually move on to something else. I mean, everyone at school is a bit on edge at certain times—exam season, inspections, when the photocopier jams five minutes before a lesson, which is practically a weekly event—but I wouldn’t say I walk around feeling anxious all the time.\n\nI can get a bit keyed up, I suppose, but I’m not sure if that’s anxiety or just being busy. What do you mean by “on edge,” exactly?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do things in a certain way or repeat certain actions?\n\nPatient: Not really, I don’t think so. I mean, I have routines, like most teachers do—if you don’t have some sort of system, the whole day falls apart, especially with thirty pupils all needing something at once—but I wouldn’t say I have to do things a certain way or repeat things.\n\nI might double-check I’ve got my keys or my register, but that feels more like being practical than anything. Sorry, I’ve gone off a bit. No, I haven’t noticed any repeating actions like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose I do—though not in a dramatic way. It’s more in conversations. Like at book club, or curriculum meetings at school, I’ll suddenly jump in before someone’s actually finished what they’re saying because the answer is already there in my head and out it comes.\n\nIt’s embarrassing sometimes, because I don’t mean to be rude. I’ll think, “Oh, for goodness’ sake, let them finish,” but by then I’ve already said it. It happens more when I’m interested in the topic, actually—if we’re discussing a novel or planning a scheme of work, I get carried along with it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting with your hands or feet when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do, actually. Not constantly, but enough that I notice it. If I’m sitting at my desk marking essays, I’ll realise my foot’s been tapping away for ages, or I’m clicking my pen without thinking—which is dreadful if someone else is in the room, because it’s the sort of thing that would drive me mad if a pupil were doing it.\n\nAnd in church, when I’m meant to be sitting still and listening properly, I’ll find I’m shifting about in the pew or moving my feet, and then I sort of tell myself, “Right, settle down.” It’s not deliberate, it just seems to happen when I’m sitting still for too long.\n\nDoctor: Are these behaviors, like getting up during meetings or fidgeting, causing problems for you at work or in your relationships with others?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it has started to, a bit. Not in a catastrophic way, but people do lose patience with me sometimes, especially in meetings at work. I can see it on their faces when I’ve jumped in again or when I’m shifting about instead of just sitting quietly.\n\nAnd at book club too, which sounds silly, because that’s meant to be enjoyable—we were discussing a novel last month and I kept coming in too quickly, and afterwards I went home feeling embarrassed rather than, you know, part of things. Connected, I suppose. Same with people in the community sometimes. I don’t mean any harm, but I can leave feeling I’ve irritated everyone.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these behaviors are affecting your overall well-being or causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it does, though I almost feel silly saying that because it’s not as if anything dreadful has happened. But it does wear on me.\n\nIt’s more the after-effect, really. I’ll come away from a staff meeting or book club thinking, “Why did I say that like that?” or “Why couldn’t I just wait?” And then I feel embarrassed, and a bit separate from people, instead of feeling I’ve joined in properly. People do lose patience with me sometimes — not always openly, but you can tell, can’t you?\n\nIt’s affected things at work and in the community a bit, I’d say. I used to really enjoy those sorts of discussions, and I still do, but now I sometimes dread that I’ll irritate everyone again.\n\nDoctor: Do you often struggle to follow through on instructions or finish tasks, such as lesson plans or grading assignments?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, actually. I mean, with lesson plans and marking, I do finish them because one has to, don’t you? I haven’t particularly thought of it that way. Sometimes I’ll lose my thread if there’s a lot happening, but then in school there always is a lot happening. cultural center, giving three examples when one would do, and then I feel embarrassed when I see people trying to wrap it up.\n- key: S018\n  name: Interrupting_Intruding\n  description: Often interrupts or intrudes on others (e.g., butts into conversations, games, or activities).\n  manifestation: In group discussions I often jump in before someone has finished their thought, especially at book club or curriculum meetings, because the response is already in my head and I blurt it out before I can stop myself.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: Onset prior to age 12 years\n  manifestation: Even as a girl, my report cards said I was bright but could not stay in my seat or wait my turn, and my mother used to joke that I wore a path between my desk and the pencil sharpener before I was ten.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: It has started to affect my relationships at work and in my community because people lose patience with me in meetings and book club, and I leave feeling embarrassed instead of connected.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 8 months\n  manifestation: This has been noticeably harder to manage for about eight months, since my youngest left for college and the house became quieter.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 60 / secondary_school_teacher / graduate\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S011\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty staying seated and fidgeting is more related to feeling restless or having trouble paying attention?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more a restlessness thing, probably. It’s not always that I’ve stopped listening exactly — though I suppose if I’m walking to the back of the room, I’m not fully sitting with it, am I?\n\nIt’s more that in meetings, or even at dinner when everyone else is perfectly settled, I suddenly feel I ought to get up and do something. Refill the coffee, check whether there are enough handouts, move something at the side of the room… and then afterwards I think, “Was that actually necessary?” Usually it wasn’t urgent at all.\n\nIt’s a bit embarrassing, really, because other people can just stay put, and I’m up and down like I’m hosting a village fête.\n\nDoctor: Do you often make impulsive decisions without fully considering the consequences?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call them impulsive decisions, exactly. I mean, I can decide things rather quickly sometimes — in a staffroom sort of way, you know, “Yes, let’s move that lesson to Thursday,” or “Oh, I’ll volunteer for that,” and then later I remember my diary is already full of tiny little traps.\n\nBut serious decisions… I don’t think I’m reckless. I’m not off buying cars or booking flights to Peru on a whim — though Peru would be wonderful, wouldn’t it, all those textiles and the mountains. We did a unit once where a student brought in something her grandmother had woven, and I remember thinking—\n\nSorry, I’m wandering. I suppose I haven’t really noticed myself making big decisions without thinking through the consequences. Smaller things, perhaps, but I don’t know that it’s a major problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you have more trouble with staying focused on tasks or with sitting still and being patient?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the sitting still and being patient side of it. I can focus, at least sometimes, if I’m properly engaged, but what brought me in really was this business of getting up when everyone else is seated.\n\nIn meetings I’ll suddenly think, “Oh, I should refill the coffee,” or “I’d better check those handouts,” and I’m halfway out of my chair before I’ve really considered whether it needs doing. Or at dinner, I’ll pop up to move something or fetch something, and everyone else is just… calmly sitting there like normal adults.\n\nSometimes I’ll walk to the back of the room as if there’s a purpose, but there often isn’t anything urgent. It’s more like I can’t quite remain planted. Then afterwards I feel a bit silly, because it probably could have waited.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think your mind often wanders off when you're supposed to be listening to someone or doing a task?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "patient_turn_index: 15\n(If 1, this is your first reply — return your chief complaint.)\nReturn the JSON now."
  }

{"key":"None","name":"None","description":"None","reason":"The question is about mind-wandering or difficulty sustaining attention while listening or doing tasks, which is an inattentive symptom not covered by the provided hyperactive/impulsive symptom profile items."}

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure, really. I mean, everyone’s mind wanders sometimes, doesn’t it? Especially if someone is explaining a timetable change for the third time and there’s a poster peeling off the wall behind them — I always notice things like that.

But whether it’s often… I don’t know. I haven’t particularly noticed it as the main thing. I can usually follow someone if I’m trying to, though I might drift a little and then come back. It’s hard to judge, isn’t it, because you only notice once you’ve already wandered.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.8, F90.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose the main thing is I’ve noticed I can’t seem to stay seated when I’m meant to. Like in staff meetings, or even at dinner, everyone else is sitting there and I’ll suddenly get up to refill coffee, or check a handout, or wander to the back of the room. It feels like I need to do something, even though when I think about it, it’s not really urgent.\n\nI’m a teacher, so meetings are part of life, unfortunately—sorry, that’s not really the point—but it’s been happening enough that I’ve started wondering why I keep doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring thoughts that you can't seem to get out of your head?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you mean, anyway. I mean, I’ll have a lot of things bouncing around in my head, especially school things—marking, emails, who needs what form signed—but I wouldn’t say it’s one thought that gets stuck and won’t leave.\n\nThough now I’m wondering if you mean worries? Because I can get preoccupied with little practical things, like whether I remembered to lock the classroom cupboard, but then I’ll usually move on to something else. Sorry, I’m not sure if I’m answering that properly.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or on edge?\n\nPatient: Anxious… I don’t know, not especially? I mean, everyone at school is a bit on edge at certain times—exam season, inspections, when the photocopier jams five minutes before a lesson, which is practically a weekly event—but I wouldn’t say I walk around feeling anxious all the time.\n\nI can get a bit keyed up, I suppose, but I’m not sure if that’s anxiety or just being busy. What do you mean by “on edge,” exactly?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do things in a certain way or repeat certain actions?\n\nPatient: Not really, I don’t think so. I mean, I have routines, like most teachers do—if you don’t have some sort of system, the whole day falls apart, especially with thirty pupils all needing something at once—but I wouldn’t say I have to do things a certain way or repeat things.\n\nI might double-check I’ve got my keys or my register, but that feels more like being practical than anything. Sorry, I’ve gone off a bit. No, I haven’t noticed any repeating actions like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose I do—though not in a dramatic way. It’s more in conversations. Like at book club, or curriculum meetings at school, I’ll suddenly jump in before someone’s actually finished what they’re saying because the answer is already there in my head and out it comes.\n\nIt’s embarrassing sometimes, because I don’t mean to be rude. I’ll think, “Oh, for goodness’ sake, let them finish,” but by then I’ve already said it. It happens more when I’m interested in the topic, actually—if we’re discussing a novel or planning a scheme of work, I get carried along with it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting with your hands or feet when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do, actually. Not constantly, but enough that I notice it. If I’m sitting at my desk marking essays, I’ll realise my foot’s been tapping away for ages, or I’m clicking my pen without thinking—which is dreadful if someone else is in the room, because it’s the sort of thing that would drive me mad if a pupil were doing it.\n\nAnd in church, when I’m meant to be sitting still and listening properly, I’ll find I’m shifting about in the pew or moving my feet, and then I sort of tell myself, “Right, settle down.” It’s not deliberate, it just seems to happen when I’m sitting still for too long.\n\nDoctor: Are these behaviors, like getting up during meetings or fidgeting, causing problems for you at work or in your relationships with others?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it has started to, a bit. Not in a catastrophic way, but people do lose patience with me sometimes, especially in meetings at work. I can see it on their faces when I’ve jumped in again or when I’m shifting about instead of just sitting quietly.\n\nAnd at book club too, which sounds silly, because that’s meant to be enjoyable—we were discussing a novel last month and I kept coming in too quickly, and afterwards I went home feeling embarrassed rather than, you know, part of things. Connected, I suppose. Same with people in the community sometimes. I don’t mean any harm, but I can leave feeling I’ve irritated everyone.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these behaviors are affecting your overall well-being or causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it does, though I almost feel silly saying that because it’s not as if anything dreadful has happened. But it does wear on me.\n\nIt’s more the after-effect, really. I’ll come away from a staff meeting or book club thinking, “Why did I say that like that?” or “Why couldn’t I just wait?” And then I feel embarrassed, and a bit separate from people, instead of feeling I’ve joined in properly. People do lose patience with me sometimes — not always openly, but you can tell, can’t you?\n\nIt’s affected things at work and in the community a bit, I’d say. I used to really enjoy those sorts of discussions, and I still do, but now I sometimes dread that I’ll irritate everyone again.\n\nDoctor: Do you often struggle to follow through on instructions or finish tasks, such as lesson plans or grading assignments?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, actually. I mean, with lesson plans and marking, I do finish them because one has to, don’t you? The pupils are coming in whether I’m ready or not, and the essays can’t mark themselves—more’s the pity.\n\nI suppose I can get a bit waylaid, but I don’t know if it’s more than anyone else. Sometimes I’ll sit down to do one thing and end up sorting a pile of papers or remembering I need to email someone about a school trip—well, not even a trip, more like one of those assemblies we all get roped into—and then I think, “What was I meant to be doing?”\n\nBut I wouldn’t want to say I often don’t follow through on instructions. I’m not sure that’s the main thing, really.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted by things around you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, really. I mean, I notice things going on around me — in a classroom you rather have to, don’t you? A child rummaging in a bag, someone at the window, the radiator making that dreadful clanking noise we still haven’t had fixed.\n\nBut whether I’m more distracted than other people… I don’t know. I haven’t particularly thought of it that way. Sometimes I’ll lose my thread if there’s a lot happening, but then in school there always is a lot happening. Even the corridors have their own weather, almost — noise, coats everywhere, someone needing a form signed.\n\nSo… I’m not sure I’d say it’s a big thing I’ve noticed, no.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself talking more than others in social situations?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I do, actually. Not all the time, but I’ve noticed it particularly in the Spanish department, or at the cultural centre, where we’ll be discussing something quite ordinary and I somehow end up giving three examples when one would have done.\n\nIt’s funny, because I don’t set out to dominate anything. I just get caught up — like if we’re talking about a film screening, I’ll start mentioning the director, and then something about an article I read, and then a student who once said something relevant, and by then I can see someone glancing at their watch or doing that polite little “right, anyway” face.\n\nAnd then afterwards I feel embarrassed. I think, “Why didn’t I just stop sooner?” So yes, I suppose I can take over a conversation more than I mean to.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty staying seated and fidgeting is more related to feeling restless or having trouble paying attention?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more a restlessness thing, probably. It’s not always that I’ve stopped listening exactly — though I suppose if I’m walking to the back of the room, I’m not fully sitting with it, am I?\n\nIt’s more that in meetings, or even at dinner when everyone else is perfectly settled, I suddenly feel I ought to get up and do something. Refill the coffee, check whether there are enough handouts, move something at the side of the room… and then afterwards I think, “Was that actually necessary?” Usually it wasn’t urgent at all.\n\nIt’s a bit embarrassing, really, because other people can just stay put, and I’m up and down like I’m hosting a village fête.\n\nDoctor: Do you often make impulsive decisions without fully considering the consequences?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call them impulsive decisions, exactly. I mean, I can decide things rather quickly sometimes — in a staffroom sort of way, you know, “Yes, let’s move that lesson to Thursday,” or “Oh, I’ll volunteer for that,” and then later I remember my diary is already full of tiny little traps.\n\nBut serious decisions… I don’t think I’m reckless. I’m not off buying cars or booking flights to Peru on a whim — though Peru would be wonderful, wouldn’t it, all those textiles and the mountains. We did a unit once where a student brought in something her grandmother had woven, and I remember thinking—\n\nSorry, I’m wandering. I suppose I haven’t really noticed myself making big decisions without thinking through the consequences. Smaller things, perhaps, but I don’t know that it’s a major problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you have more trouble with staying focused on tasks or with sitting still and being patient?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the sitting still and being patient side of it. I can focus, at least sometimes, if I’m properly engaged, but what brought me in really was this business of getting up when everyone else is seated.\n\nIn meetings I’ll suddenly think, “Oh, I should refill the coffee,” or “I’d better check those handouts,” and I’m halfway out of my chair before I’ve really considered whether it needs doing. Or at dinner, I’ll pop up to move something or fetch something, and everyone else is just… calmly sitting there like normal adults.\n\nSometimes I’ll walk to the back of the room as if there’s a purpose, but there often isn’t anything urgent. It’s more like I can’t quite remain planted. Then afterwards I feel a bit silly, because it probably could have waited.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your mind often wanders off when you're supposed to be listening to someone or doing a task?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, really. I haven’t particularly noticed it as the main thing. I can usually follow someone if I’m trying to, though I might drift a little and then come back. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

I’m not sure that’s the main thing, really.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted by things around you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, really. I mean, I notice things going on around me — in a classroom you rather have to, don’t you? A child rummaging in a bag, someone at the window, the radiator making that dreadful clanking noise we still haven’t had fixed.\n\nBut whether I’m more distracted than other people… I don’t know. I haven’t particularly thought of it that way. Sometimes I’ll lose my thread if there’s a lot happening, but then in school there always is a lot happening. Even the corridors have their own weather, almost — noise, coats everywhere, someone needing a form signed.\n\nSo… I’m not sure I’d say it’s a big thing I’ve noticed, no.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself talking more than others in social situations?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I do, actually. Not all the time, but I’ve noticed it particularly in the Spanish department, or at the cultural centre, where we’ll be discussing something quite ordinary and I somehow end up giving three examples when one would have done.\n\nIt’s funny, because I don’t set out to dominate anything. I just get caught up — like if we’re talking about a film screening, I’ll start mentioning the director, and then something about an article I read, and then a student who once said something relevant, and by then I can see someone glancing at their watch or doing that polite little “right, anyway” face.\n\nAnd then afterwards I feel embarrassed. I think, “Why didn’t I just stop sooner?” So yes, I suppose I can take over a conversation more than I mean to.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty staying seated and fidgeting is more related to feeling restless or having trouble paying attention?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more a restlessness thing, probably. It’s not always that I’ve stopped listening exactly — though I suppose if I’m walking to the back of the room, I’m not fully sitting with it, am I?\n\nIt’s more that in meetings, or even at dinner when everyone else is perfectly settled, I suddenly feel I ought to get up and do something. Refill the coffee, check whether there are enough handouts, move something at the side of the room… and then afterwards I think, “Was that actually necessary?” Usually it wasn’t urgent at all.\n\nIt’s a bit embarrassing, really, because other people can just stay put, and I’m up and down like I’m hosting a village fête.\n\nDoctor: Do you often make impulsive decisions without fully considering the consequences?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call them impulsive decisions, exactly. I mean, I can decide things rather quickly sometimes — in a staffroom sort of way, you know, “Yes, let’s move that lesson to Thursday,” or “Oh, I’ll volunteer for that,” and then later I remember my diary is already full of tiny little traps.\n\nBut serious decisions… I don’t think I’m reckless. I’m not off buying cars or booking flights to Peru on a whim — though Peru would be wonderful, wouldn’t it, all those textiles and the mountains. We did a unit once where a student brought in something her grandmother had woven, and I remember thinking—\n\nSorry, I’m wandering. I suppose I haven’t really noticed myself making big decisions without thinking through the consequences. Smaller things, perhaps, but I don’t know that it’s a major problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you have more trouble with staying focused on tasks or with sitting still and being patient?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the sitting still and being patient side of it. I can focus, at least sometimes, if I’m properly engaged, but what brought me in really was this business of getting up when everyone else is seated.\n\nIn meetings I’ll suddenly think, “Oh, I should refill the coffee,” or “I’d better check those handouts,” and I’m halfway out of my chair before I’ve really considered whether it needs doing. Or at dinner, I’ll pop up to move something or fetch something, and everyone else is just… calmly sitting there like normal adults.\n\nSometimes I’ll walk to the back of the room as if there’s a purpose, but there often isn’t anything urgent. It’s more like I can’t quite remain planted. Then afterwards I feel a bit silly, because it probably could have waited.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your mind often wanders off when you're supposed to be listening to someone or doing a task?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, really. I mean, everyone’s mind wanders sometimes, doesn’t it? Especially if someone is explaining a timetable change for the third time and there’s a poster peeling off the wall behind them — I always notice things like that.\n\nBut whether it’s often… I don’t know. I haven’t particularly noticed it as the main thing. I can usually follow someone if I’m trying to, though I might drift a little and then come back. It’s hard to judge, isn’t it, because you only notice once you’ve already wandered.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think it’s more like an excess of energy in my body than not paying attention, if that makes sense. I can be listening, but my foot is going under the desk like a sewing machine.

When I’m grading essays at my desk, for instance, I’ll catch myself tapping my foot or clicking my pen, and then I suddenly think, “Oh, stop that, that’s probably maddening.” Or in church, which is possibly the worst place for it because everything is so quiet, I’ll realise I’ve been shifting about in the pew and have to tell myself to sit properly.

It’s not usually because I don’t know what’s happening around me. It’s more that I have to keep reminding my body to settle down.

I used to really enjoy those sorts of discussions, and I still do, but now I sometimes dread that I’ll irritate everyone again.\n\nDoctor: Do you often struggle to follow through on instructions or finish tasks, such as lesson plans or grading assignments?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, actually. I mean, with lesson plans and marking, I do finish them because one has to, don’t you? The pupils are coming in whether I’m ready or not, and the essays can’t mark themselves—more’s the pity.\n\nI suppose I can get a bit waylaid, but I don’t know if it’s more than anyone else. Sometimes I’ll sit down to do one thing and end up sorting a pile of papers or remembering I need to email someone about a school trip—well, not even a trip, more like one of those assemblies we all get roped into—and then I think, “What was I meant to be doing?”\n\nBut I wouldn’t want to say I often don’t follow through on instructions. I’m not off buying cars or booking flights to Peru on a whim — though Peru would be wonderful, wouldn’t it, all those textiles and the mountains. I suppose I haven’t really noticed myself making big decisions without thinking through the consequences. Smaller things, perhaps, but I don’t know that it’s a major problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you have more trouble with staying focused on tasks or with sitting still and being patient?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the sitting still and being patient side of it. I can focus, at least sometimes, if I’m properly engaged, but what brought me in really was this business of getting up when everyone else is seated.\n\nIn meetings I’ll suddenly think, “Oh, I should refill the coffee,” or “I’d better check those handouts,” and I’m halfway out of my chair before I’ve really considered whether it needs doing. Or at dinner, I’ll pop up to move something or fetch something, and everyone else is just… calmly sitting there like normal adults.\n\nSometimes I’ll walk to the back of the room as if there’s a purpose, but there often isn’t anything urgent. Then afterwards I feel a bit silly, because it probably could have waited.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your mind often wanders off when you're supposed to be listening to someone or doing a task?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, really. Especially if someone is explaining a timetable change for the third time and there’s a poster peeling off the wall behind them — I always notice things like that.\n\nBut whether it’s often… I don’t know. I haven’t particularly noticed it as the main thing. I can usually follow someone if I’m trying to, though I might drift a little and then come back. It’s hard to judge, isn’t it, because you only notice once you’ve already wandered.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty with sitting still and fidgeting is more closely related to feeling overly energetic or to having trouble paying attention to what's going on around you?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more like an excess of energy in my body than not paying attention, if that makes sense. I can be listening, but my foot is going under the desk like a sewing machine.\n\nWhen I’m grading essays at my desk, for instance, I’ll catch myself tapping my foot or clicking my pen, and then I suddenly think, “Oh, stop that, that’s probably maddening.” Or in church, which is possibly the worst place for it because everything is so quiet, I’ll realise I’ve been shifting about in the pew and have to tell myself to sit properly.\n\nIt’s not usually because I don’t know what’s happening around me. I don’t mean any harm, but I can leave feeling I’ve irritated everyone.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these behaviors are affecting your overall well-being or causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it does, though I almost feel silly saying that because it’s not as if anything dreadful has happened. I used to really enjoy those sorts of discussions, and I still do, but now I sometimes dread that I’ll irritate everyone again.\n\nDoctor: Do you often struggle to follow through on instructions or finish tasks, such as lesson plans or grading assignments?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, actually. I mean, with lesson plans and marking, I do finish them because one has to, don’t you? I haven’t particularly thought of it that way. Sometimes I’ll lose my thread if there’s a lot happening, but then in school there always is a lot happening. I just get caught up — like if we’re talking about a film screening, I’ll start mentioning the director, and then something about an article I read, and then a student who once said something relevant, and by then I can see someone glancing at their watch or doing that polite little “right, anyway” face.\n\nAnd then afterwards I feel embarrassed. I think, “Why didn’t I just stop sooner?” So yes, I suppose I can take over a conversation more than I mean to.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty staying seated and fidgeting is more related to feeling restless or having trouble paying attention?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more a restlessness thing, probably. Yes, I think so. I mean, my mother certainly thought so. She used to joke that before I was ten I’d worn a little path between my desk and the pencil sharpener, because I was up and down so much.
